From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop noticing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It shows up to me in every photo, every reflection… even when people say I look fine, it feels really obvious. It’s gotten to the point where I’m avoiding presentations and meeting with clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your appearance in mirrors or reflections throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot. Before work or any family event, I can get stuck for hours in the bathroom mirror. I’ll take pictures on my phone from different angles, redo my makeup, adjust my scarf, then check again.\n\nI ask my husband over and over if my face looks lopsided. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs to see how bad mine looks. It’s exhausting, but I still keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or urges that aren't about your appearance, that you feel compelled to act on or can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stuck on the face thing, but I can’t think of other urges or thoughts like that that aren’t about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and jaw affected your relationships or daily activities beyond just avoiding presentations and meeting with clients?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gone beyond work meetings. I actually cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I couldn’t handle people looking at my face.\n\nI’ve also been skipping networking events connected to an award I was supposed to be involved with. I just can’t face being seen. And now my project deadlines are slipping, and even family gatherings are starting to fall apart because I either don’t go or I’m so distracted by how I look that I can’t really be present.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concerns about your face and jaw are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s causing me a lot of distress. I feel like I can’t stop seeing this droop or unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo, every mirror, even little reflections.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, but it just feels so obvious to me. It’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations and clients because I can’t stand the idea of people looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw when they look at you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know people tell me they don’t see it, but when I look at photos or even my reflection, the droop around my mouth and jaw looks really obvious to me. So when someone is looking at me, I just assume they can see it too, and that’s why I avoid presentations or meeting with clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your face and jaw actually look normal, and it's just your perception that's off?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I try to tell myself maybe it looks normal, because people keep saying they don’t see anything. But honestly, when I look at photos or catch my reflection, I can’t stop seeing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It feels so obvious to me that it’s hard to believe it’s just my perception. That’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw are not as noticeable to others as they are to you?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s possible, because people do keep telling me I look fine. But to me, I can’t stop seeing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections. It feels so obvious that I have trouble believing other people don’t notice it, and that’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the droop and unevenness you see are not actually there?\n\nPatient: I guess there’s a possibility, because people keep telling me I look fine. But when I see myself in photos or in a reflection, I can’t not see it—the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw just looks so obvious to me. It’s hard for me to believe it isn’t really there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw if a doctor or a plastic surgeon told you that there was nothing wrong with your face?\n\nPatient: I think I probably would still see it. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw in every photo and reflection. The droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw feels so obvious to me, and it’s gotten to where I avoid presentations and clients because I’m so focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any amount of evidence or reassurance that could convince you that the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw are not actually there?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don’t know.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop noticing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It shows up to me in every photo, every reflection… even when people say I look fine, it feels really obvious. It’s gotten to the point where I’m avoiding presentations and meeting with clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your appearance in mirrors or reflections throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot. Before work or any family event, I can get stuck for hours in the bathroom mirror. I’ll take pictures on my phone from different angles, redo my makeup, adjust my scarf, then check again.\n\nI ask my husband over and over if my face looks lopsided. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs to see how bad mine looks. It’s exhausting, but I still keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or urges that aren't about your appearance, that you feel compelled to act on or can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stuck on the face thing, but I can’t think of other urges or thoughts like that that aren’t about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and jaw affected your relationships or daily activities beyond just avoiding presentations and meeting with clients?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gone beyond work meetings. I actually cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I couldn’t handle people looking at my face.\n\nI’ve also been skipping networking events connected to an award I was supposed to be involved with. I just can’t face being seen. And now my project deadlines are slipping, and even family gatherings are starting to fall apart because I either don’t go or I’m so distracted by how I look that I can’t really be present.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concerns about your face and jaw are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s causing me a lot of distress. I feel like I can’t stop seeing this droop or unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo, every mirror, even little reflections.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, but it just feels so obvious to me. It’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations and clients because I can’t stand the idea of people looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw when they look at you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know people tell me they don’t see it, but when I look at photos or even my reflection, the droop around my mouth and jaw looks really obvious to me. So when someone is looking at me, I just assume they can see it too, and that’s why I avoid presentations or meeting with clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your face and jaw actually look normal, and it's just your perception that's off?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I try to tell myself maybe it looks normal, because people keep saying they don’t see anything. But honestly, when I look at photos or catch my reflection, I can’t stop seeing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It feels so obvious to me that it’s hard to believe it’s just my perception. That’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw are not as noticeable to others as they are to you?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s possible, because people do keep telling me I look fine. But to me, I can’t stop seeing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections. It feels so obvious that I have trouble believing other people don’t notice it, and that’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the droop and unevenness you see are not actually there?\n\nPatient: I guess there’s a possibility, because people keep telling me I look fine. But when I see myself in photos or in a reflection, I can’t not see it—the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw just looks so obvious to me. It’s hard for me to believe it isn’t really there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw if a doctor or a plastic surgeon told you that there was nothing wrong with your face?\n\nPatient: I think I probably would still see it. I mean, even when people tell me I look fine, I look at a photo or catch my reflection and the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw just feels so obvious. That’s why I’m here—it’s gotten to the point where I’m avoiding presentations and clients because I can’t stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of the droop and unevenness is completely inaccurate, and that there's actually nothing wrong with your face?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s possible, but it’s really hard for me to believe that. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw in every photo and reflection. Even when people tell me I look fine, it feels so obvious to me that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Would you still want to undergo a cosmetic procedure to fix the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw even if multiple doctors told you that it was not noticeable and didn't need correction?\n\nPatient: I think I still would want something done, yes. I know that sounds extreme if doctors were saying it wasn’t noticeable, but I can’t stop seeing it in photos and reflections. The droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw feels so obvious to me, and it’s gotten to where I avoid presentations and clients because I’m so focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any amount of evidence or reassurance that could convince you that the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw are not actually there?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don’t know. I want to say yes, but even when people reassure me, I still see it right away in photos or mirrors. The droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw just looks so obvious to me. It’s why I’m here, because I can’t stop focusing on it, and it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your face and jaw caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affecting things at home and with people. I’ve been skipping family gatherings because I can’t deal with being looked at, and that’s caused tension. Work has been falling apart too — I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute, and I’ve missed networking events for an award because I couldn’t face being seen. My deadlines are slipping, and it’s putting strain on everyone around me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It shows up to me in every photo, every reflection… even when people say I look fine, it feels really obvious. It’s gotten to the point where I’m avoiding presentations and meeting with clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your appearance in mirrors or reflections throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot. Before work or any family event, I can get stuck for hours in the bathroom mirror. I’ll take pictures on my phone from different angles, redo my makeup, adjust my scarf, then check again.\n\nI ask my husband over and over if my face looks lopsided. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs to see how bad mine looks. It’s exhausting, but I still keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or urges that aren't about your appearance, that you feel compelled to act on or can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stuck on the face thing, but I can’t think of other urges or thoughts like that that aren’t about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and jaw affected your relationships or daily activities beyond just avoiding presentations and meeting with clients?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gone beyond work meetings. I actually cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I couldn’t handle people looking at my face.\n\nI’ve also been skipping networking events connected to an award I was supposed to be involved with. I just can’t face being seen. And now my project deadlines are slipping, and even family gatherings are starting to fall apart because I either don’t go or I’m so distracted by how I look that I can’t really be present.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concerns about your face and jaw are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s causing me a lot of distress. I feel like I can’t stop seeing this droop or unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo, every mirror, even little reflections.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, but it just feels so obvious to me. It’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations and clients because I can’t stand the idea of people looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw when they look at you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know people tell me they don’t see it, but when I look at photos or even my reflection, the droop around my mouth and jaw looks really obvious to me. So when someone is looking at me, I just assume they can see it too, and that’s why I avoid presentations or meeting with clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your face and jaw actually look normal, and it's just your perception that's off?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I try to tell myself maybe it looks normal, because people keep saying they don’t see anything. But honestly, when I look at photos or catch my reflection, I can’t stop seeing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It feels so obvious to me that it’s hard to believe it’s just my perception. That’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw are not as noticeable to others as they are to you?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s possible, because people do keep telling me I look fine. But to me, I can’t stop seeing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections. It feels so obvious that I have trouble believing other people don’t notice it, and that’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the droop and unevenness you see are not actually there?\n\nPatient: I guess there’s a possibility, because people keep telling me I look fine. But when I see myself in photos or in a reflection, I can’t not see it—the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw just looks so obvious to me. It’s hard for me to believe it isn’t really there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw if a doctor or a plastic surgeon told you that there was nothing wrong with your face?\n\nPatient: I think I probably would still see it. I mean, even when people tell me I look fine, I look at a photo or catch my reflection and the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw just feels so obvious. That’s why I’m here—it’s gotten to the point where I’m avoiding presentations and clients because I can’t stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of the droop and unevenness is completely inaccurate, and that there's actually nothing wrong with your face?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s possible, but it’s really hard for me to believe that. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw in every photo and reflection. Even when people tell me I look fine, it feels so obvious to me that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Would you still want to undergo a cosmetic procedure to fix the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw even if multiple doctors told you that it was not noticeable and didn't need correction?\n\nPatient: I think I still would want something done, yes. I know that sounds extreme if doctors were saying it wasn’t noticeable, but I can’t stop seeing it in photos and reflections. The droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw feels so obvious to me, and it’s gotten to where I avoid presentations and clients because I’m so focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any amount of evidence or reassurance that could convince you that the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw are not actually there?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don’t know. I want to say yes, but even when people reassure me, I still see it right away in photos or mirrors. The droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw just looks so obvious to me. It’s why I’m here, because I can’t stop focusing on it, and it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your face and jaw caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affecting things at home and with people. I’ve been skipping family gatherings because I can’t deal with being looked at, and that’s caused tension. Work has been falling apart too — I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute, and I’ve missed networking events for an award because I couldn’t face being seen. My deadlines are slipping, and it’s putting strain on everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your view of your face and jaw could change if you received consistent reassurance from people you trust?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would, honestly. I do trust the people saying I look fine, but when I see a photo or catch my reflection, the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw just jumps out at me. It feels so obvious, and I can’t stop focusing on it. That’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s why I’m here, because I can’t stop focusing on it, and it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your face and jaw caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affecting things at home and with people. I’ve been skipping family gatherings because I can’t deal with being looked at, and that’s caused tension. Work has been falling apart too — I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute, and I’ve missed networking events for an award because I couldn’t face being seen. My deadlines are slipping, and it’s putting strain on everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your view of your face and jaw could change if you received consistent reassurance from people you trust?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would, honestly. A lot. Before work or any family event, I can get stuck for hours in the bathroom mirror. I’ll take pictures on my phone from different angles, redo my makeup, adjust my scarf, then check again.\n\nI ask my husband over and over if my face looks lopsided. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs to see how bad mine looks. It’s exhausting, but I still keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or urges that aren't about your appearance, that you feel compelled to act on or can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stuck on the face thing, but I can’t think of other urges or thoughts like that that aren’t about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and jaw affected your relationships or daily activities beyond just avoiding presentations and meeting with clients?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gone beyond work meetings. I actually cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I couldn’t handle people looking at my face.\n\nI’ve also been skipping networking events connected to an award I was supposed to be involved with. I just can’t face being seen. And now my project deadlines are slipping, and even family gatherings are starting to fall apart because I either don’t go or I’m so distracted by how I look that I can’t really be present.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concerns about your face and jaw are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s causing me a lot of distress. I feel like I can’t stop seeing this droop or unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo, every mirror, even little reflections.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, but it just feels so obvious to me. It’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations and clients because I can’t stand the idea of people looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw when they look at you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know people tell me they don’t see it, but when I look at photos or even my reflection, the droop around my mouth and jaw looks really obvious to me. So when someone is looking at me, I just assume they can see it too, and that’s why I avoid presentations or meeting with clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your face and jaw actually look normal, and it's just your perception that's off?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I try to tell myself maybe it looks normal, because people keep saying they don’t see anything. But honestly, when I look at photos or catch my reflection, I can’t stop seeing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It feels so obvious to me that it’s hard to believe it’s just my perception. That’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw are not as noticeable to others as they are to you?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s possible, because people do keep telling me I look fine. But to me, I can’t stop seeing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw, especially in photos or reflections. It feels so obvious that I have trouble believing other people don’t notice it, and that’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the droop and unevenness you see are not actually there?\n\nPatient: I guess there’s a possibility, because people keep telling me I look fine. But when I see myself in photos or in a reflection, I can’t not see it—the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw just looks so obvious to me. It’s hard for me to believe it isn’t really there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw if a doctor or a plastic surgeon told you that there was nothing wrong with your face?\n\nPatient: I think I probably would still see it. I mean, even when people tell me I look fine, I look at a photo or catch my reflection and the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw just feels so obvious. That’s why I’m here—it’s gotten to the point where I’m avoiding presentations and clients because I can’t stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of the droop and unevenness is completely inaccurate, and that there's actually nothing wrong with your face?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s possible, but it’s really hard for me to believe that. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw in every photo and reflection. Even when people tell me I look fine, it feels so obvious to me that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Would you still want to undergo a cosmetic procedure to fix the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw even if multiple doctors told you that it was not noticeable and didn't need correction?\n\nPatient: I think I still would want something done, yes. I know that sounds extreme if doctors were saying it wasn’t noticeable, but I can’t stop seeing it in photos and reflections. The droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw feels so obvious to me, and it’s gotten to where I avoid presentations and clients because I’m so focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any amount of evidence or reassurance that could convince you that the droop and unevenness around your mouth and jaw are not actually there?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don’t know. I want to say yes, but even when people reassure me, I still see it right away in photos or mirrors. The droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw just looks so obvious to me. It’s why I’m here, because I can’t stop focusing on it, and it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your face and jaw caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affecting things at home and with people. I’ve been skipping family gatherings because I can’t deal with being looked at, and that’s caused tension. Work has been falling apart too — I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute, and I’ve missed networking events for an award because I couldn’t face being seen. My deadlines are slipping, and it’s putting strain on everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your view of your face and jaw could change if you received consistent reassurance from people you trust?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would, honestly. I do trust the people saying I look fine, but when I see a photo or catch my reflection, the droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw just jumps out at me. It feels so obvious, and I can’t stop focusing on it. That’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you think any amount of professional confirmation that your face is normal could ever completely change your perception of it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it could completely change it. I mean, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw in every photo and reflection. Even when people tell me I look fine, it still feels so obvious to me. That’s why I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance constantly even if you were completely alone with no one to see you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. It’s not only about other people seeing me. Before work or a family event, I can spend hours in the bathroom mirror, taking pictures of myself from different angles, fixing my makeup or my scarf, trying to make my face look less lopsided. I’ll ask my husband over and over if it looks uneven. And sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing my face to theirs and feeling like mine looks worse.\n\nDoctor: Has your focus on your face and jaw interfered with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gotten in the way a lot. I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face being seen like that. And there have been award-related networking events I should have gone to, but I’ve been missing them for the same reason. My project deadlines are starting to slip, and even family gatherings feel like they’re falling apart because I don’t want people looking at my face.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
